PREFACE 


Tae Duka-Patthana is one of the several compila- 
tions termed Patthānas, which, taken together, are 
known to Buddhists as Patthānapakaranay, or 
Mahapakaranay. It has been transcribed for the 
Pali Text Society through the unflagging and careful 
labour of my friend Miss G. M. Noakes and my sister, 
Miss M. C. Foley, B.Sc., during 1904-5. Their transcrip- 
tion is from the Siamese edition, with which I have 
collated—(1) the Burmese Mandalay palmleaf MS. belong- 
ing to the India Office Library; (2) a paper transcript 
in Sinhalese character, copied at Colombo for Professor 
Rhys Davids some twenty years ago; and (8) the fine 
edition published by the Hanthawaddy Press at Rangoon, 
and lent me from his own library by the Rev. Ananda 
Metteyya, the English founder of the Buddhasāsana 
Samagama. Owing to the fact that Pali scholars, in 
cataloguing the Buddhist canon, had given the Duka- 
patthana priority over the rest of the work (see J.P.T.S., 
1882, pp. 34, 61; 1896-1908, p. 33); and also as, in the 
Siamese volumes containing the work, no order of sequence 
was indicated, this part was handed over for transcription 
first, without further investigation. In the work of collation, 
however, the number of peyydlas seemed to refer to 
passages given in full elsewhere. The Mandalay MS., 
moreover, besides being often scarcely legible through 


want of ink, proved to be not infrequently faulty; and 
ix 


X Preface. 


the Sinhalese copy was worse. I therefore wrote to the 
Rev. Ananda Metteyya begging him to order for me a 
eopy of the entire work from the Hanthawaddy Press. 
With the greatest kindness he dispatched to me his own 
copy. But there were unfortunate delays, so that the five 
volumes only reached me when several sheets were await- 
ing final collation and revision. I then noted that the 
volumes inseribed first and second were devoted to the 
Tikapatthāna, the third to the Dukapatthāna. 
The same order is observed in the Mātikā of the 
Dhammasaygani and in the Paūhapuccehakas 
ofthe Vibhanga. The Paficappakarana-attha- 
kathā and the Atthasālinī, p. 9, confirm it in the 
case of the Patthāna. It was, however, too late to do 
more than start the transcription of the Tikapatthāna. 
There is, nevertheless, no progressive development in the 
gubject-matter of either division of the work which might 
put the reader, if he begin with the second (Duka- 
patthāna), at a disadvantage. On the contrary, there 
is & certain advantage in coping with the relative sim- 
plicity of the logical combinations of pairs (positive and 
negative), before proceeding to the more elaborate combina- 
tions of triplets of terms. There remains, nevertheless, 
the very positive disadvantage of the peyy àla's occurring 
in the Dukapatthàna from the very outset, and 
referring to recurring formula given in full, some of them 
very lengthy, in the Tikapatthāna. These I have 
transcribed from the Rangoon edition and placed in an 
Appendix—a method that will prove, at least, not more 
cumbersome to the reader than if, to fill up the lacunes left 
in all these texts, he had to fetch down from his shelves a 
prior edition of the Tikapatthāna. 

There remains the fact of certain minor but frequent 
excisions of my own, made not without some fear and 
trembling. The inordinate length and symmetrical struc- 
ture of the work, coupled with the very attenuated 
interest it can claim to possess for any modern student, 
whether of the West or of the East, would go far to 


Preface. xi 


justify & much more extended condensation than has 
been made. And the whole of the Patthāna being 
practically nothing more than a huge series of logical 
exercises in the correlating of terms, to be acquired, 
originally, by oral teaching, it becomes as superfluous to 
print more than the variable portion in each formula as it 
would be to give in full what is summed ina mathematical 
symbol, or in such set phrases as those in “The House that 
Jack built. Even, however, if the advisability of condensa- 
tion be admitted, the “where” and the “how much” of it is 
an anxious problem. The old story of the father and his son 
carrying, and being carried by, the donkey, and never 
any way escaping the blame of passers-by, has scarcely been 
absent from my mind for the space of six months. The 
result is that my own excisions do not exceed and seldom 
equal those in the Burmese and Sinhalese texts. And, 
except in the case of the abbreviations in the nine numbered 
and recurring formule, the reader, if he hold it worth while, 
may consult the footnote references. In my own judgment, 
the remainder of the Patthana could very well undergo 
such a surplus of condensation as would enable the whole 
of it to be edited in two more volumes. There are still, no 
doubt, pious and erudite Abhidhammikas spending 
secluded lives in mastering these interminable permuta- 
tions of possible modes of subjective "experience. But . 
for the majority of Buddhists, as for students of other 
persuasions, the Pali Text Society's editions aim more, I 
take ii, at being a medium of enlightenment, in which the 
unessential elaborations of form are reckoned as relatively 
unimportant, beside the high mission of conveying, to the 
greatest possible number, the vital structure in a great 
phase of human thought. 


1 Were it not for the relative fullness in the Siamese 
edition, the task of editing the Patthana would be 
fraught with more difficulty. The other texts, to a great 
extent, give only the chief terms in each sentence. Cf. p. 9, 
n.; p. 181, nn. 


xii Preface. 


To those, nevertheless, who, on opening this volume 
about the middle, find its perusal too irritating, I would 
suggest, first, a preliminary study of the appended table of 
ihe nine formule, recurring uniformly in every Duka; 
and next, of the first Duka, taken in conjunction with 
the Appendix. Or of the twelfth Duka (Lokiya), to 
which the texts frequently refer, when abbreviating, as if 
to a sort of norm. 

Respecting the first Duka, or rather the first group of 
Dukas, it should always be remembered that the term 
hetu is used in its Abhidhamma, or more technical 
sense, a8 meaning the three basic or ‘ ground’ conditions 
of bad karma—lust, enmity, and illasion—and of their 
three opposites—viz., alobha, adosa, amoha—as 
stated in the Hetugocchaka of the Dhamma- 
saygani (pp. 188-192). I do not think Childers dis- 
tinguished this more special meaning under either ‘Hetu’ 
or ‘Patthanayn’ in his dictionary. If the more popular 
meaning of the word as ‘ cause,’ or ‘reason, so familiar to 
us in the Sutta-Pitaka phrase, ‘tan kissa hetu?' 
—the cadence calling up the Irish idiom “And for why ?'— 
be relegated to the words in the text, ‘paticca’ and 
‘arabbha,’ it will at least render more intelligible the 
opening pages, not to say much in those that follow. Thus 
in the opening sentence of the Hetuduka we may read, 
for hetu, not ‘cause,’ nor for dhammo “thing,” but 
by hetu dhammo understand that one of these six 


1 I do not mean that dhamma, elsewhere in the 
Dhamma, is not often best rendered by ‘thing,’ or 
‘quality,’ but that its Abhidhamma sense seems to 
be more strictly, or more often, ‘state of mind,’ or “of 
consciousness.’ See my ‘ Psychological Ethics,’ xxxii-xxxv., 
and Index to Sanyutta Nikaya (vol. vi), s. v. Dhamma. 
I must apologize for the Germanized expression, ‘ ground- 
state.’ The Buddhists call it mula, root (of good, bad 
karma). 


Preface. xiii 


well-defined states of ethical experience is meant. For | 
paccaya, again, we may take, not the particular relation of 
cause, but the more general meaning of ‘relation.’ This 
relieves the sentence of the congestion in causal terminology, 
and we get :—‘ Under-the-[ given ]-relation of ground-state :— 
because of one ground-state another ground-state arises. 

[For instance] absence of lust induces absence of enmity 
and of infatuation.” 

Our formula is now revealed as a fairly simple and 
(pace Childers) unmetaphysical statement of ethical doc- 
trines, not more (if not less) metaphysical, at least, than 
such statements as Petruchio’s :—‘ It (over-roasted flesh) 
engenders choler, planteth anger.’ 

The sample of the present work which Childers quotes 
(sv. Patthànar) is from the, to us, vaguer relation, 
n&hetu-paccaya, and occurs on p. 4, $5, of this volume. 
It is less easy to render in more intelligible English than is 
his. But this is largely owing to the logical love in the 
mind of the first Buddhist scholasties? for the use of con- 
trary and contradictory forms. Bearing this in mind, and 

repeating the alterations made above, we get :— Under the 
[given]relation of non-ground-state:—because of what is not 
a ground-state of mind a ground-state of mind arises.’ [For 
instance] because of skandhas [say, a condition of body 
and mind], accompanied by doubt and excitement, infatua- 
tion arises also accompanied by doubt and excitement.” The 
pupil was taught to consider four of the five skandhas 
which made up his individuality as n a-h etu, as in them- 
selves ethically neutral. Hence to him the category 
nahetu or ahetuka easily suggested skandhas. 


1 More literally, ‘ muddleheadedness.' 

2 We note its root in the use of negative forms with 
positive import—e.g., alobho, etc. See op. cit., p. 21, n. 
8, 92, n. 3. 

3 Vibhanga, p. 68. The skandha of syntheses 
(sankhara), in so far as it meant states of mind 
elaborated by citta, could include the ground-states. 
Cf. Dh. S., 30, 80, ete. 


xiv Preface. 


None the less, the text remains very difficult and 
obscure to the uninitiated Western mind, and I am far 
from pretending to solve any one of its problems. I 
only suggest that, from what I can discover, the simpler, 
less metaphysical reading usually proves the more lucid ; 
and again, that the aim of the work seems to have 
been more a series of exercises in a logic of terms and 
relations than any attempt to enunciate ‘metaphysical 
propositions.’ The student who had mastered the doctrines, 
laid down and illustrated with picturesque detail in the 
Nikayas, was, in these Abhidhamma catechisms 
and exercises, led on to practise himself in analyses of the 
modes and conditioned procedure of ethical consciousness. 
He was to gain insight into, and mastery over, the working 
of his own subjective experience, by letting the mind play 
about among these correlations of terms, that were to him 
richer in import than to us. 

All further discussion of patthana, paccaya, and 
the like, I propose to defer till an edition of the Tika- 
patthana sees its way to the light. Here let it suffice 
to add that, in provisionally using, above, ‘relation’ for 
paccaya, I wished to avoid any risk of restricting that 
term, which plays so wide a part in this work, to any 
exact coincidence of meaning with our terms ‘ condition,’ 
‘ antecedent,’ or ‘cause,’ which might prove eventually too 
narrow for it. The Commentary warns me herein by — 
defining paccaya as any upakarako dhammo— 
any state, or term, let us say, which, in a specified variety 
of ways (see, e.g., list on p. 3) is ancillary to another term. 

It only remains for me here and now to tender my 
very cordial thanks to the Rev. Ananda Metteyya for his 
prompt and generous co-operation in my labours, and also 
my grateful acknowledgment of the courtesy of Mr. F. W. 
Thomas in lending me the Mandalay MS. 


C. A. F. RHYS DAVIDS. 
November, 1906.

APPENDIX 


THERE are in the foregoing Duka's a certain number of 
lacung which the texts apparently require the reader to fill 
up from the first part, or Tikapatthàna. I have transcribed 
ceríain passages, as they occur for the first time in the 
Tikapatthāna, from the several vàra's corresponding to 
those in the Hetuduka, in which those lacune first occur. 
These passages recur, of course, and require to be adapted 
to the varying terms of the particular Duka’s. Thus, for 
instance, all allusion to kusala, akusala, avyakata 
(the subject-matter of the first Tika) must be altered. 
This is done in the case of the second, or Vedana Tika, but 
the main features remain as in the Kusalattika. 
Of the first lacuna, p. 1, $1:—Patisandhikkhane 
„pe... I can only point out that, as appears 
iro about the work, the contents of the patisandhi 
sentences vary at each occurrence. And it is probable 
that, where only... pe... is given, the instance, or 
instances immediately preceding those sentences are to be 
understood as applying also to whatever modifying condition, 
presumably rebirth, is introduced by that term.) [Kd.] 


1 At its first occurrence in the Tikapatthana the Com- 
mentary says :—Patisandhikkhane ti katatta rupa- 
saņkhatassa avyākatassa avyakatapaticca uppatti das- 
sanatthay vuttay. The formula, namely, under which 
it first occurs, is Avyākatay dhammar paticca avyakato 
dhammo uppajjati hetupaccaya.

It will be seen that, with material so unwieldy as the last 
guotation, the problem of how to fill in the slight skeletons 
in the various Duka's is only answered by the new problem 
of what not to put in. A comparison of the skeletons with 
the filling-in matter may go some way to solve it. On 
p. 14 the words catutthassa maggassa should 


866 Appendix. 


apparently have been inserted before minay jappeti. 
Maggassa, again, in the following section, as well as 
patthanāya, seem by the foregoing to be out of their 
place after senásana&r. This is again the case in 
the second Duka. The Lokiya-Duka, on the other hand 
(p. 142), gives, in its upanissayavāra, a rather more 
consecutive rendering of parts of the original, though it 
still remains diffieult to know how much to insert imme- 
diately before the expression manan jappeti 

A curious feature in the variants under B-E is that, in the 
present volume, ariya almost entirely (cf. p. 15) replaces 
araha. Once or twice, in this connexion, one of the 
texts wil give the verb (usually paccavekkhati) in 
the singular, with Ariya for subject (e.g., p. 348, l 1), 
as if the Arahā of the Tikas had become Ariya in the 
Duka's gradually, not to say inadvertently. (In Burmese 
the h and y are easily confused.)  [Ed.]
